Yes… I think I am, a bit. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds silly, but in the summer I do plan things around where I think wasps might be.

Even at home, if there’s buzzing near the back porch, I’ll ask my husband to unload things instead of me going out there. I know that’s not exactly a big social event, but it’s all tied up with avoiding being outside where they might be. Does that answer what you mean?

I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it seems like such a small thing, but it has changed what I do.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social situations because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am, a bit. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds silly, but in the summer I do plan things around where I think wasps might be.\n\nI’ve stopped doing some of the garden jobs at church, which is partly social, really, because I’m not there with the others like I used to be. And if people are gathering outside, I’ll sometimes make excuses or stay more on the edges if I’ve heard buzzing around.\n\nEven at home, if there’s buzzing near the back porch, I’ll ask my husband to unload things instead of me going out there. I know that’s not exactly a big social event, but it’s all tied up with avoiding being outside where they might be. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s gotten a little out of hand, maybe. I’m here because I’ve started planning my whole summer around trying to avoid wasps. I know that probably sounds silly, but I’ll even skip helping with the garden jobs at church, and if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I make my husband unload things instead. It’s just… I don’t want it running things quite so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it’s not out of nowhere exactly—it’s when I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch or see one hovering near the flowers. My heart sort of jumps right away, and my hands get shaky, and I feel like I need to get inside immediately. It happens pretty fast. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this kind of fear in response to anything else, or is it mainly just wasps that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly just wasps, I think. I mean, I don’t like other bugs much, but it’s not the same. With wasps… I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just wave it away, but if one gets close to me, it feels like something awful is about to happen. I know that sounds a bit dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships or daily activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I’ve stepped back from the church gardening group, and I stopped hosting those garden days at my house. That used to be one of the ways I stayed in touch with people.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has been doing more of the porch and garden things I used to just handle myself. He’s kind about it, but… I know it’s not really fair to him. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about wasps when you're not near them or in a situation where they might be present?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, if I’m inside or it’s winter or something, I’m not sitting there thinking about them all day. Sometimes if I know I have to go outside, I might wonder if there’ll be any around, but… I don’t know if that counts. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a picture of a wasp or even just thinking about one trigger a fear response in you?\n\nPatient: A picture… not so much, I don’t think. I might feel a little uneasy, but it’s not like when there’s a real one near me.\n\nIf I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch or see one hovering around the flowers, that’s when it happens right away — my heart sort of jumps, my hands get shaky, and I just feel like I need to get inside. I know that probably sounds silly, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps ever occur when you are completely alone with your thoughts, without any external cue like seeing or hearing a wasp?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. If I’m just sitting by myself, with nothing happening, I’m not usually thinking about wasps or getting frightened out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more if something reminds me, I suppose — like going outside or hearing a buzz. But completely alone with my thoughts? I haven’t really noticed that, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about wasps when you are not near them ever trigger the same level of fear as when you see or hear one?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it gets to the same level just from thinking about them when I’m not near one. I might feel a little uncomfortable if the thought comes up, but it’s not that sudden fear like when I actually see one or hear the buzzing.\n\nI hope that makes sense… it’s more real when it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides gardening and hosting events, where your fear of wasps is causing problems or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose it has spread a little beyond just gardening or having people over, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI’ve stepped back from the church gardening group, which is a shame because that was one of the ways I kept connected with people. And at home, my husband has taken over some of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it seems like such a small thing, but it has changed what I do.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social situations because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am, a bit. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds silly, but in the summer I do plan things around where I think wasps might be.\n\nI’ve stopped doing some of the garden jobs at church, which is partly social, really, because I’m not there with the others like I used to be. And if people are gathering outside, I’ll sometimes make excuses or stay more on the edges if I’ve heard buzzing around.\n\nEven at home, if there’s buzzing near the back porch, I’ll ask my husband to unload things instead of me going out there. I know that’s not exactly a big social event, but it’s all tied up with avoiding being outside where they might be. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other things that are not directly related to wasps?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding other things.\n\nIt’s mostly just the wasps, or places where I think they might be. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds silly, but in the summer I do plan things around where I think wasps might be.\n\nI’ve stopped doing some of the garden jobs at church, which is partly social, really, because I’m not there with the others like I used to be. And if people are gathering outside, I’ll sometimes make excuses or stay more on the edges if I’ve heard buzzing around.\n\nEven at home, if there’s buzzing near the back porch, I’ll ask my husband to unload things instead of me going out there. I know that’s not exactly a big social event, but it’s all tied up with avoiding being outside where they might be. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other things that are not directly related to wasps?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding other things.\n\nIt’s mostly just the wasps, or places where I think they might be. I’m not suddenly afraid of insects in general or anything like that… unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you started to fear or avoid other insects or animals that are similar to wasps, like bees?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I know that probably sounds silly, but I’ll even skip helping with the garden jobs at church, and if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I make my husband unload things instead. It’s just… I don’t want it running things quite so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it’s not out of nowhere exactly—it’s when I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch or see one hovering near the flowers. My heart sort of jumps right away, and my hands get shaky, and I feel like I need to get inside immediately. It happens pretty fast. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this kind of fear in response to anything else, or is it mainly just wasps that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly just wasps, I think. I mean, I don’t like other bugs much, but it’s not the same. With wasps… I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just wave it away, but if one gets close to me, it feels like something awful is about to happen. I know that sounds a bit dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships or daily activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I’ve stepped back from the church gardening group, and I stopped hosting those garden days at my house. That used to be one of the ways I stayed in touch with people.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has been doing more of the porch and garden things I used to just handle myself. He’s kind about it, but… I know it’s not really fair to him. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about wasps when you're not near them or in a situation where they might be present?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, if I’m inside or it’s winter or something, I’m not sitting there thinking about them all day. Sometimes if I know I have to go outside, I might wonder if there’ll be any around, but… I don’t know if that counts. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a picture of a wasp or even just thinking about one trigger a fear response in you?\n\nPatient: A picture… not so much, I don’t think. I might feel a little uneasy, but it’s not like when there’s a real one near me.\n\nIf I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch or see one hovering around the flowers, that’s when it happens right away — my heart sort of jumps, my hands get shaky, and I just feel like I need to get inside. I know that probably sounds silly, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps ever occur when you are completely alone with your thoughts, without any external cue like seeing or hearing a wasp?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose… not really in winter, no, or not in a sensible way. I know it sounds a bit daft, because a wasp is only a small insect and most people would just wave it away or swat it.

But if I think there might be one near me, even somewhere it’s probably unlikely, I get this feeling like something awful is about to happen. I do know that’s out of proportion, I really do. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?

That used to be one of the ways I stayed in touch with people.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has been doing more of the porch and garden things I used to just handle myself. He’s kind about it, but… I know it’s not really fair to him. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about wasps when you're not near them or in a situation where they might be present?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, if I’m inside or it’s winter or something, I’m not sitting there thinking about them all day. Sometimes if I know I have to go outside, I might wonder if there’ll be any around, but… I don’t know if that counts. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a picture of a wasp or even just thinking about one trigger a fear response in you?\n\nPatient: A picture… not so much, I don’t think. I might feel a little uneasy, but it’s not like when there’s a real one near me.\n\nIf I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch or see one hovering around the flowers, that’s when it happens right away — my heart sort of jumps, my hands get shaky, and I just feel like I need to get inside. I know that probably sounds silly, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps ever occur when you are completely alone with your thoughts, without any external cue like seeing or hearing a wasp?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. If I’m just sitting by myself, with nothing happening, I’m not usually thinking about wasps or getting frightened out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more if something reminds me, I suppose — like going outside or hearing a buzz. But completely alone with my thoughts? I haven’t really noticed that, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about wasps when you are not near them ever trigger the same level of fear as when you see or hear one?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it gets to the same level just from thinking about them when I’m not near one. I might feel a little uncomfortable if the thought comes up, but it’s not that sudden fear like when I actually see one or hear the buzzing.\n\nI hope that makes sense… it’s more real when it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides gardening and hosting events, where your fear of wasps is causing problems or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose it has spread a little beyond just gardening or having people over, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI’ve stepped back from the church gardening group, which is a shame because that was one of the ways I kept connected with people. And at home, my husband has taken over some of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it seems like such a small thing, but it has changed what I do.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social situations because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am, a bit. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds silly, but in the summer I do plan things around where I think wasps might be.\n\nI’ve stopped doing some of the garden jobs at church, which is partly social, really, because I’m not there with the others like I used to be. And if people are gathering outside, I’ll sometimes make excuses or stay more on the edges if I’ve heard buzzing around.\n\nEven at home, if there’s buzzing near the back porch, I’ll ask my husband to unload things instead of me going out there. I know that’s not exactly a big social event, but it’s all tied up with avoiding being outside where they might be. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other things that are not directly related to wasps?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding other things.\n\nIt’s mostly just the wasps, or places where I think they might be. I’m not suddenly afraid of insects in general or anything like that… unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other insects or animals that are similar to wasps, like bees?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Bees don’t really bother me in the same way, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, if something is buzzing very close to me I might step away, but I don’t think I’m avoiding bees or other animals the way I do with wasps. It’s mostly that particular worry. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might see a wasp in places where they are unlikely to be, such as in your house or in the winter?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not really in winter, no, or not in a sensible way. I know it sounds a bit daft, because a wasp is only a small insect and most people would just wave it away or swat it.\n\nBut if I think there might be one near me, even somewhere it’s probably unlikely, I get this feeling like something awful is about to happen. I do know that’s out of proportion, I really do. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps ever triggered by something that reminds you of wasps, but isn't actually a wasp, such as a picture or a story about wasps?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think a picture or someone telling a story would set me off in the same way as an actual wasp being near me.\n\nI might not love talking about them, but I wouldn’t say I avoid pictures or anything like that. At least, I haven’t noticed that happening. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have a similar fear response to something that looks or sounds like a wasp, but is not actually a wasp?\n\nPatient: Maybe if it sounded enough like one, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but the second I hear that sharp sort of buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps and my hands can shake a bit.\n\nEven before I’m sure what it is, I feel like I need to get inside right away. I know that’s probably more than most people would feel, but that’s what happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I suppose it has affected things a bit more than I’d like to admit.

I might feel a little uneasy, but it’s not like when there’s a real one near me.\n\nIf I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch or see one hovering around the flowers, that’s when it happens right away — my heart sort of jumps, my hands get shaky, and I just feel like I need to get inside. I know that probably sounds silly, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps ever occur when you are completely alone with your thoughts, without any external cue like seeing or hearing a wasp?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. If I’m just sitting by myself, with nothing happening, I’m not usually thinking about wasps or getting frightened out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more if something reminds me, I suppose — like going outside or hearing a buzz. But completely alone with my thoughts? I haven’t really noticed that, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about wasps when you are not near them ever trigger the same level of fear as when you see or hear one?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it gets to the same level just from thinking about them when I’m not near one. I might feel a little uncomfortable if the thought comes up, but it’s not that sudden fear like when I actually see one or hear the buzzing.\n\nI hope that makes sense… it’s more real when it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides gardening and hosting events, where your fear of wasps is causing problems or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose it has spread a little beyond just gardening or having people over, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI’ve stepped back from the church gardening group, which is a shame because that was one of the ways I kept connected with people. And at home, my husband has taken over some of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it seems like such a small thing, but it has changed what I do.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social situations because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am, a bit. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds silly, but in the summer I do plan things around where I think wasps might be.\n\nI’ve stopped doing some of the garden jobs at church, which is partly social, really, because I’m not there with the others like I used to be. And if people are gathering outside, I’ll sometimes make excuses or stay more on the edges if I’ve heard buzzing around.\n\nEven at home, if there’s buzzing near the back porch, I’ll ask my husband to unload things instead of me going out there. I know that’s not exactly a big social event, but it’s all tied up with avoiding being outside where they might be. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other things that are not directly related to wasps?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding other things.\n\nIt’s mostly just the wasps, or places where I think they might be. I’m not suddenly afraid of insects in general or anything like that… unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other insects or animals that are similar to wasps, like bees?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Bees don’t really bother me in the same way, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, if something is buzzing very close to me I might step away, but I don’t think I’m avoiding bees or other animals the way I do with wasps. It’s mostly that particular worry. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might see a wasp in places where they are unlikely to be, such as in your house or in the winter?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not really in winter, no, or not in a sensible way. I know it sounds a bit daft, because a wasp is only a small insect and most people would just wave it away or swat it.\n\nBut if I think there might be one near me, even somewhere it’s probably unlikely, I get this feeling like something awful is about to happen. I do know that’s out of proportion, I really do. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps ever triggered by something that reminds you of wasps, but isn't actually a wasp, such as a picture or a story about wasps?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think a picture or someone telling a story would set me off in the same way as an actual wasp being near me.\n\nI might not love talking about them, but I wouldn’t say I avoid pictures or anything like that. At least, I haven’t noticed that happening. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have a similar fear response to something that looks or sounds like a wasp, but is not actually a wasp?\n\nPatient: Maybe if it sounded enough like one, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but the second I hear that sharp sort of buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps and my hands can shake a bit.\n\nEven before I’m sure what it is, I feel like I need to get inside right away. I know that’s probably more than most people would feel, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways, besides the ones you've mentioned, that your fear of wasps is affecting your daily life or causing you distress?\n\nPatient: I suppose it has affected things a bit more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI used to be quite involved with the church gardening group, and I’ve stepped back from that. And I don’t really host garden days at home anymore, which is a shame, because that was one of the ways I kept up with people.\n\nAt home, my husband tends to do the porch and garden jobs now, the things I would’ve just done myself before. I feel a bit silly saying it, because it’s not as if I’m helpless, but it has changed things. Sometimes if I know I have to go outside, I might wonder if there’ll be any around, but… I don’t know if that counts. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a picture of a wasp or even just thinking about one trigger a fear response in you?\n\nPatient: A picture… not so much, I don’t think. I might feel a little uneasy, but it’s not like when there’s a real one near me.\n\nIf I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch or see one hovering around the flowers, that’s when it happens right away — my heart sort of jumps, my hands get shaky, and I just feel like I need to get inside. I know that probably sounds silly, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps ever occur when you are completely alone with your thoughts, without any external cue like seeing or hearing a wasp?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. If I’m just sitting by myself, with nothing happening, I’m not usually thinking about wasps or getting frightened out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more if something reminds me, I suppose — like going outside or hearing a buzz. But completely alone with my thoughts? I haven’t really noticed that, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about wasps when you are not near them ever trigger the same level of fear as when you see or hear one?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it gets to the same level just from thinking about them when I’m not near one. I might feel a little uncomfortable if the thought comes up, but it’s not that sudden fear like when I actually see one or hear the buzzing.\n\nI hope that makes sense… it’s more real when it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides gardening and hosting events, where your fear of wasps is causing problems or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose it has spread a little beyond just gardening or having people over, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI’ve stepped back from the church gardening group, which is a shame because that was one of the ways I kept connected with people. And at home, my husband has taken over some of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it seems like such a small thing, but it has changed what I do.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social situations because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am, a bit. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds silly, but in the summer I do plan things around where I think wasps might be.\n\nI’ve stopped doing some of the garden jobs at church, which is partly social, really, because I’m not there with the others like I used to be. And if people are gathering outside, I’ll sometimes make excuses or stay more on the edges if I’ve heard buzzing around.\n\nEven at home, if there’s buzzing near the back porch, I’ll ask my husband to unload things instead of me going out there. I know that’s not exactly a big social event, but it’s all tied up with avoiding being outside where they might be. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other things that are not directly related to wasps?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding other things.\n\nIt’s mostly just the wasps, or places where I think they might be. I’m not suddenly afraid of insects in general or anything like that… unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other insects or animals that are similar to wasps, like bees?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Bees don’t really bother me in the same way, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, if something is buzzing very close to me I might step away, but I don’t think I’m avoiding bees or other animals the way I do with wasps. It’s mostly that particular worry. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might see a wasp in places where they are unlikely to be, such as in your house or in the winter?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not really in winter, no, or not in a sensible way. I know it sounds a bit daft, because a wasp is only a small insect and most people would just wave it away or swat it.\n\nBut if I think there might be one near me, even somewhere it’s probably unlikely, I get this feeling like something awful is about to happen. I do know that’s out of proportion, I really do. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps ever triggered by something that reminds you of wasps, but isn't actually a wasp, such as a picture or a story about wasps?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think a picture or someone telling a story would set me off in the same way as an actual wasp being near me.\n\nI might not love talking about them, but I wouldn’t say I avoid pictures or anything like that. At least, I haven’t noticed that happening. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have a similar fear response to something that looks or sounds like a wasp, but is not actually a wasp?\n\nPatient: Maybe if it sounded enough like one, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but the second I hear that sharp sort of buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps and my hands can shake a bit.\n\nEven before I’m sure what it is, I feel like I need to get inside right away. I know that’s probably more than most people would feel, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways, besides the ones you've mentioned, that your fear of wasps is affecting your daily life or causing you distress?\n\nPatient: I suppose it has affected things a bit more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI used to be quite involved with the church gardening group, and I’ve stepped back from that. And I don’t really host garden days at home anymore, which is a shame, because that was one of the ways I kept up with people.\n\nAt home, my husband tends to do the porch and garden jobs now, the things I would’ve just done myself before. I feel a bit silly saying it, because it’s not as if I’m helpless, but it has changed things. Is that the sort of effect you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps ever triggered by just thinking about wasps when you are in a situation where you know there are no wasps present?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. If I’m sitting inside or somewhere I know there aren’t any wasps, just thinking about them doesn’t usually set me off.\n\nI mean, I don’t particularly enjoy thinking about them, obviously, but it’s not the same as when I hear that buzzing or see one nearby. My heart sort of jumps right away, and my hands get shaky, and I feel like I need to get inside immediately. It happens pretty fast. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this kind of fear in response to anything else, or is it mainly just wasps that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly just wasps, I think. I mean, I don’t like other bugs much, but it’s not the same. With wasps… I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just wave it away, but if one gets close to me, it feels like something awful is about to happen. I know that sounds a bit dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships or daily activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I’ve stepped back from the church gardening group, and I stopped hosting those garden days at my house. That used to be one of the ways I stayed in touch with people.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has been doing more of the porch and garden things I used to just handle myself. He’s kind about it, but… I know it’s not really fair to him. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about wasps when you're not near them or in a situation where they might be present?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, if I’m inside or it’s winter or something, I’m not sitting there thinking about them all day. Sometimes if I know I have to go outside, I might wonder if there’ll be any around, but… I don’t know if that counts. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a picture of a wasp or even just thinking about one trigger a fear response in you?\n\nPatient: A picture… not so much, I don’t think. I might feel a little uneasy, but it’s not like when there’s a real one near me.\n\nIf I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch or see one hovering around the flowers, that’s when it happens right away — my heart sort of jumps, my hands get shaky, and I just feel like I need to get inside. I know that probably sounds silly, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps ever occur when you are completely alone with your thoughts, without any external cue like seeing or hearing a wasp?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. If I’m just sitting by myself, with nothing happening, I’m not usually thinking about wasps or getting frightened out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more if something reminds me, I suppose — like going outside or hearing a buzz. But completely alone with my thoughts? I haven’t really noticed that, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about wasps when you are not near them ever trigger the same level of fear as when you see or hear one?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it gets to the same level just from thinking about them when I’m not near one. I might feel a little uncomfortable if the thought comes up, but it’s not that sudden fear like when I actually see one or hear the buzzing.\n\nI hope that makes sense… it’s more real when it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides gardening and hosting events, where your fear of wasps is causing problems or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose it has spread a little beyond just gardening or having people over, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI’ve stepped back from the church gardening group, which is a shame because that was one of the ways I kept connected with people. And at home, my husband has taken over some of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it seems like such a small thing, but it has changed what I do.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social situations because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am, a bit. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds silly, but in the summer I do plan things around where I think wasps might be.\n\nI’ve stopped doing some of the garden jobs at church, which is partly social, really, because I’m not there with the others like I used to be. And if people are gathering outside, I’ll sometimes make excuses or stay more on the edges if I’ve heard buzzing around.\n\nEven at home, if there’s buzzing near the back porch, I’ll ask my husband to unload things instead of me going out there. I know that’s not exactly a big social event, but it’s all tied up with avoiding being outside where they might be. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other things that are not directly related to wasps?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding other things.\n\nIt’s mostly just the wasps, or places where I think they might be. I’m not suddenly afraid of insects in general or anything like that… unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other insects or animals that are similar to wasps, like bees?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Bees don’t really bother me in the same way, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, if something is buzzing very close to me I might step away, but I don’t think I’m avoiding bees or other animals the way I do with wasps. It’s mostly that particular worry. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might see a wasp in places where they are unlikely to be, such as in your house or in the winter?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not really in winter, no, or not in a sensible way. I know it sounds a bit daft, because a wasp is only a small insect and most people would just wave it away or swat it.\n\nBut if I think there might be one near me, even somewhere it’s probably unlikely, I get this feeling like something awful is about to happen. I do know that’s out of proportion, I really do. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps ever triggered by something that reminds you of wasps, but isn't actually a wasp, such as a picture or a story about wasps?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think a picture or someone telling a story would set me off in the same way as an actual wasp being near me.\n\nI might not love talking about them, but I wouldn’t say I avoid pictures or anything like that. At least, I haven’t noticed that happening. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have a similar fear response to something that looks or sounds like a wasp, but is not actually a wasp?\n\nPatient: Maybe if it sounded enough like one, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but the second I hear that sharp sort of buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps and my hands can shake a bit.\n\nEven before I’m sure what it is, I feel like I need to get inside right away. I know that’s probably more than most people would feel, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways, besides the ones you've mentioned, that your fear of wasps is affecting your daily life or causing you distress?\n\nPatient: I suppose it has affected things a bit more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI used to be quite involved with the church gardening group, and I’ve stepped back from that. And I don’t really host garden days at home anymore, which is a shame, because that was one of the ways I kept up with people.\n\nAt home, my husband tends to do the porch and garden jobs now, the things I would’ve just done myself before. I feel a bit silly saying it, because it’s not as if I’m helpless, but it has changed things. Is that the sort of effect you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps ever triggered by just thinking about wasps when you are in a situation where you know there are no wasps present?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. If I’m sitting inside or somewhere I know there aren’t any wasps, just thinking about them doesn’t usually set me off.\n\nI mean, I don’t particularly enjoy thinking about them, obviously, but it’s not the same as when I hear that buzzing or see one nearby. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel your fear of wasps is causing you significant distress or problems that we haven't discussed yet?\n\nPatient: I think the main things are the ones I mentioned, really. I’ve pulled back from the church gardening group, and I’ve stopped doing those garden days at home, which used to be a nice way for me to see people.\n\nAnd then around the house, my husband has taken over the porch and garden bits I used to do myself. I know that may not sound huge, but it does bother me a bit, losing that independence. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not work, since I’m not working now, but socially… yes, a bit.

I suppose I’ve started planning most of the summer around avoiding places where wasps might be. I’ve already mentioned the church gardening, but that’s probably the biggest one socially, because I’ve more or less given that up. And if we’re having things delivered or bringing shopping in by the back porch and I hear buzzing, I’ll ask my husband to do it instead.

It sounds a bit ridiculous when I say it out loud, but I do find myself arranging things so I don’t have to deal with them. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s gotten a little out of hand, maybe. I’m here because I’ve started planning my whole summer around trying to avoid wasps. I know that probably sounds silly, but I’ll even skip helping with the garden jobs at church, and if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I make my husband unload things instead. It’s just… I don’t want it running things quite so much.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a sudden feeling of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, it’s not out of nowhere exactly—it’s when I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch or see one hovering near the flowers. My heart sort of jumps right away, and my hands get shaky, and I feel like I need to get inside immediately. It happens pretty fast. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience this kind of fear in response to anything else, or is it mainly just wasps that trigger it?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly just wasps, I think. I mean, I don’t like other bugs much, but it’s not the same. With wasps… I know it’s just a little insect, and most people would probably just wave it away, but if one gets close to me, it feels like something awful is about to happen. I know that sounds a bit dramatic.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships or daily activities in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but I’ve stepped back from the church gardening group, and I stopped hosting those garden days at my house. That used to be one of the ways I stayed in touch with people.\n\nAnd at home, my husband has been doing more of the porch and garden things I used to just handle myself. He’s kind about it, but… I know it’s not really fair to him. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about wasps when you're not near them or in a situation where they might be present?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… not really in a big way, I don’t think. I mean, if I’m inside or it’s winter or something, I’m not sitting there thinking about them all day. Sometimes if I know I have to go outside, I might wonder if there’ll be any around, but… I don’t know if that counts. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does seeing a picture of a wasp or even just thinking about one trigger a fear response in you?\n\nPatient: A picture… not so much, I don’t think. I might feel a little uneasy, but it’s not like when there’s a real one near me.\n\nIf I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch or see one hovering around the flowers, that’s when it happens right away — my heart sort of jumps, my hands get shaky, and I just feel like I need to get inside. I know that probably sounds silly, but that’s the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps ever occur when you are completely alone with your thoughts, without any external cue like seeing or hearing a wasp?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. If I’m just sitting by myself, with nothing happening, I’m not usually thinking about wasps or getting frightened out of nowhere.\n\nIt’s more if something reminds me, I suppose — like going outside or hearing a buzz. But completely alone with my thoughts? I haven’t really noticed that, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about wasps when you are not near them ever trigger the same level of fear as when you see or hear one?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it gets to the same level just from thinking about them when I’m not near one. I might feel a little uncomfortable if the thought comes up, but it’s not that sudden fear like when I actually see one or hear the buzzing.\n\nI hope that makes sense… it’s more real when it’s right there.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides gardening and hosting events, where your fear of wasps is causing problems or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose it has spread a little beyond just gardening or having people over, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nI’ve stepped back from the church gardening group, which is a shame because that was one of the ways I kept connected with people. And at home, my husband has taken over some of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. I feel a bit embarrassed saying that, because it seems like such a small thing, but it has changed what I do.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social situations because of your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I am, a bit. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds silly, but in the summer I do plan things around where I think wasps might be.\n\nI’ve stopped doing some of the garden jobs at church, which is partly social, really, because I’m not there with the others like I used to be. And if people are gathering outside, I’ll sometimes make excuses or stay more on the edges if I’ve heard buzzing around.\n\nEven at home, if there’s buzzing near the back porch, I’ll ask my husband to unload things instead of me going out there. I know that’s not exactly a big social event, but it’s all tied up with avoiding being outside where they might be. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other things that are not directly related to wasps?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. At least, I haven’t really noticed myself avoiding other things.\n\nIt’s mostly just the wasps, or places where I think they might be. I’m not suddenly afraid of insects in general or anything like that… unless I’m misunderstanding what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you started to fear or avoid other insects or animals that are similar to wasps, like bees?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Bees don’t really bother me in the same way, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI mean, if something is buzzing very close to me I might step away, but I don’t think I’m avoiding bees or other animals the way I do with wasps. It’s mostly that particular worry. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that you might see a wasp in places where they are unlikely to be, such as in your house or in the winter?\n\nPatient: I suppose… not really in winter, no, or not in a sensible way. I know it sounds a bit daft, because a wasp is only a small insect and most people would just wave it away or swat it.\n\nBut if I think there might be one near me, even somewhere it’s probably unlikely, I get this feeling like something awful is about to happen. I do know that’s out of proportion, I really do. Is that the sort of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps ever triggered by something that reminds you of wasps, but isn't actually a wasp, such as a picture or a story about wasps?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, really. I don’t think a picture or someone telling a story would set me off in the same way as an actual wasp being near me.\n\nI might not love talking about them, but I wouldn’t say I avoid pictures or anything like that. At least, I haven’t noticed that happening. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have a similar fear response to something that looks or sounds like a wasp, but is not actually a wasp?\n\nPatient: Maybe if it sounded enough like one, yes. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but the second I hear that sharp sort of buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps and my hands can shake a bit.\n\nEven before I’m sure what it is, I feel like I need to get inside right away. I know that’s probably more than most people would feel, but that’s what happens.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways, besides the ones you've mentioned, that your fear of wasps is affecting your daily life or causing you distress?\n\nPatient: I suppose it has affected things a bit more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI used to be quite involved with the church gardening group, and I’ve stepped back from that. And I don’t really host garden days at home anymore, which is a shame, because that was one of the ways I kept up with people.\n\nAt home, my husband tends to do the porch and garden jobs now, the things I would’ve just done myself before. I feel a bit silly saying it, because it’s not as if I’m helpless, but it has changed things. Is that the sort of effect you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps ever triggered by just thinking about wasps when you are in a situation where you know there are no wasps present?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. If I’m sitting inside or somewhere I know there aren’t any wasps, just thinking about them doesn’t usually set me off.\n\nI mean, I don’t particularly enjoy thinking about them, obviously, but it’s not the same as when I hear that buzzing or see one nearby. I hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life where you feel your fear of wasps is causing you significant distress or problems that we haven't discussed yet?\n\nPatient: I think the main things are the ones I mentioned, really. I’ve pulled back from the church gardening group, and I’ve stopped doing those garden days at home, which used to be a nice way for me to see people.\n\nAnd then around the house, my husband has taken over the porch and garden bits I used to do myself.
